I know that might sound silly, but it’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Is it specifically this uneven spot that bothers you, or do you feel self-conscious about your appearance in general?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mostly that specific spot, yeah… the little uneven area around my nose and cheek. I mean, I don’t think I’m, like, unhappy with everything about how I look or anything. Does that make sense?\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find yourself thinking about this uneven spot at times when you're not looking in the mirror or seeing photos of yourself?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 20 / cashier / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Preoccupation_with_Appearance\nmanifestation: \"I came in because I keep getting stuck on this tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek in photos and under the store lights, and even when other people say they don't see it, it feels like it's the first thing customers and managers notice.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. I know that might sound silly, but it’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Is it specifically this uneven spot that bothers you, or do you feel self-conscious about your appearance in general?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mostly that specific spot, yeah… the little uneven area around my nose and cheek. I mean, I don’t think I’m, like, unhappy with everything about how I look or anything. It’s just in photos, or under the bright store lights, I get stuck on it and feel like customers or my managers are noticing it first, even though people tell me they don’t really see it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about this uneven spot at times when you're not looking in the mirror or seeing photos of yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. I mean, not constantly or anything, but even when I’m not looking at a mirror or a picture, I’ll catch myself thinking about that little uneven spot around my nose and cheek—especially if I’m at work under the bright lights. I start wondering if customers or my managers are noticing it first, even though people say they don’t really see it. I know it probably sounds kind of minor.\n\nDoctor: How often would you say you think about this uneven spot during a typical day?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know exactly. It’s not like every second, but probably a few times a day? Especially when I’m at work under the store lights, or if I know someone’s looking at me, I start thinking about that tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek. I kind of tell myself other people say they don’t see it, but it still feels like it’s what customers or managers notice first. Is that… is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Has thinking about this uneven spot around your nose and cheek ever interfered with your daily activities or relationships?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… not like it’s ruining everything, but at work it’s been kind of getting in the way. My manager has noticed I’m coming back from breaks late sometimes, and I guess I’ve been moving slower at the register because I get distracted thinking about it.\n\nI feel bad saying that because I don’t want it to sound like I’m not trying. I’m actually trying to get promoted, and I’m worried it’s making me look unreliable right when I need to seem dependable. Does that count as interfering?\n\nDoctor: Are you concerned about what other people think of you in general, or is it mostly about how you look?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mostly about how I look… like, not that I don’t care what people think at all, but the thing I came in for is I keep getting stuck on this really tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek. It’s worse in photos or under the store lights, and even when people tell me they don’t notice it, I still feel like it’s the first thing customers or managers see.\n\nI know that probably sounds kind of small, but… yeah, it’s mostly that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound bigger than it is, but I do get upset about it. It’s mostly when I see it in photos or under the store lights, and then I start thinking everyone can see that uneven spot around my nose and cheek.

Even when people say they don’t notice it, I still feel like it’s the first thing customers or managers are looking at. I know that probably sounds silly, but it does make me anxious sometimes.

Does that count as interfering?\n\nDoctor: Are you concerned about what other people think of you in general, or is it mostly about how you look?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mostly about how I look… like, not that I don’t care what people think at all, but the thing I came in for is I keep getting stuck on this really tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek. It’s worse in photos or under the store lights, and even when people tell me they don’t notice it, I still feel like it’s the first thing customers or managers see.\n\nI know that probably sounds kind of small, but… yeah, it’s mostly that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to stop thinking about the uneven spot around your nose and cheek, but find that you can't?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do try to stop sometimes. Like I’ll tell myself, “Okay, other people said they don’t even see it, just let it go,” but then if I’m under the store lights or I catch myself in a photo, I get stuck on it again.\n\nIt’s this tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek, and I know it probably sounds really small, but it starts feeling like it’s the first thing customers or managers notice. I don’t want to be dramatic about it, but… yeah, it’s hard to just not think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or upset when you think about this uneven spot around your nose and cheek?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound bigger than it is, but I do get upset about it. It’s mostly when I see it in photos or under the store lights, and then I start thinking everyone can see that uneven spot around my nose and cheek.\n\nEven when people say they don’t notice it, I still feel like it’s the first thing customers or managers are looking at. I know that probably sounds silly, but it does make me anxious sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to do something specific, like checking or touching, to make yourself feel better about the uneven spot around your nose and cheek?\n\nPatient: Um… yeah, I guess I do sometimes. Is that… is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Has thinking about this uneven spot around your nose and cheek ever interfered with your daily activities or relationships?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… not like it’s ruining everything, but at work it’s been kind of getting in the way. My manager has noticed I’m coming back from breaks late sometimes, and I guess I’ve been moving slower at the register because I get distracted thinking about it.\n\nI feel bad saying that because I don’t want it to sound like I’m not trying. I’m actually trying to get promoted, and I’m worried it’s making me look unreliable right when I need to seem dependable. Does that count as interfering?\n\nDoctor: Are you concerned about what other people think of you in general, or is it mostly about how you look?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mostly about how I look… like, not that I don’t care what people think at all, but the thing I came in for is I keep getting stuck on this really tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek. It’s worse in photos or under the store lights, and even when people tell me they don’t notice it, I still feel like it’s the first thing customers or managers see.\n\nI know that probably sounds kind of small, but… yeah, it’s mostly that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to stop thinking about the uneven spot around your nose and cheek, but find that you can't?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do try to stop sometimes. Like I’ll tell myself, “Okay, other people said they don’t even see it, just let it go,” but then if I’m under the store lights or I catch myself in a photo, I get stuck on it again.\n\nIt’s this tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek, and I know it probably sounds really small, but it starts feeling like it’s the first thing customers or managers notice. I don’t want to be dramatic about it, but… yeah, it’s hard to just not think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or upset when you think about this uneven spot around your nose and cheek?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound bigger than it is, but I do get upset about it. It’s mostly when I see it in photos or under the store lights, and then I start thinking everyone can see that uneven spot around my nose and cheek.\n\nEven when people say they don’t notice it, I still feel like it’s the first thing customers or managers are looking at. I know that probably sounds silly, but it does make me anxious sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to do something specific, like checking or touching, to make yourself feel better about the uneven spot around your nose and cheek?\n\nPatient: Um… yeah, I guess I do sometimes. Not like constantly, but on my breaks I’ll go into the bathroom and check my face in the mirror, kind of just to see if it looks as bad as I’m thinking.\n\nI’ll fix my hair around that side too, like maybe if it sits a certain way it won’t show as much. And… I don’t know if this sounds bad, but I catch myself comparing my face to girls who come through my line.\n\nSometimes I take longer than I mean to, and I’ve been a little late getting back to the register. I know that’s probably not great.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your face in the mirror a certain number of times to feel okay about the uneven spot?\n\nPatient: Not a certain number, no… I don’t think I’m counting it like that. It’s more like, on my breaks I’ll end up checking in the bathroom mirror until I feel like I can go back out.\n\nSometimes I fix my hair around that side too, and… I’ll compare myself to girls who come through my line, even though I know that’s probably not helpful. I don’t mean for it to take long, but sometimes I do take too long and I’m a little late getting back to the register.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I know that might sound silly, but it’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Is it specifically this uneven spot that bothers you, or do you feel self-conscious about your appearance in general?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mostly that specific spot, yeah… the little uneven area around my nose and cheek. I mean, I don’t think I’m, like, unhappy with everything about how I look or anything. I mean, not constantly or anything, but even when I’m not looking at a mirror or a picture, I’ll catch myself thinking about that little uneven spot around my nose and cheek—especially if I’m at work under the bright lights. I know it probably sounds kind of minor.\n\nDoctor: How often would you say you think about this uneven spot during a typical day?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know exactly. It’s not like every second, but probably a few times a day? My manager has noticed I’m coming back from breaks late sometimes, and I guess I’ve been moving slower at the register because I get distracted thinking about it.\n\nI feel bad saying that because I don’t want it to sound like I’m not trying. I’m actually trying to get promoted, and I’m worried it’s making me look unreliable right when I need to seem dependable. Does that count as interfering?\n\nDoctor: Are you concerned about what other people think of you in general, or is it mostly about how you look?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mostly about how I look… like, not that I don’t care what people think at all, but the thing I came in for is I keep getting stuck on this really tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek. It’s worse in photos or under the store lights, and even when people tell me they don’t notice it, I still feel like it’s the first thing customers or managers see.\n\nI know that probably sounds kind of small, but… yeah, it’s mostly that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to stop thinking about the uneven spot around your nose and cheek, but find that you can't?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do try to stop sometimes. I don’t want to be dramatic about it, but… yeah, it’s hard to just not think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or upset when you think about this uneven spot around your nose and cheek?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound bigger than it is, but I do get upset about it. I don’t mean for it to take long, but sometimes I do take too long and I’m a little late getting back to the register.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your face in the mirror to stop yourself from thinking about the uneven spot?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, kind of. I mean, not constantly or anything, but even when I’m not looking at a mirror or a picture, I’ll catch myself thinking about that little uneven spot around my nose and cheek—especially if I’m at work under the bright lights. I start wondering if customers or my managers are noticing it first, even though people say they don’t really see it. I know it probably sounds kind of minor.\n\nDoctor: How often would you say you think about this uneven spot during a typical day?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know exactly. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess it’s a little embarrassing, but I’ve been kind of stuck on this small uneven spot around my nose and cheek. It’s probably not that noticeable, and people tell me they don’t really see it, but in photos or under the lights at work, I feel like it’s the first thing customers or managers notice. I know that might sound silly, but it’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Is it specifically this uneven spot that bothers you, or do you feel self-conscious about your appearance in general?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mostly that specific spot, yeah… the little uneven area around my nose and cheek. I mean, I don’t think I’m, like, unhappy with everything about how I look or anything. It’s just in photos, or under the bright store lights, I get stuck on it and feel like customers or my managers are noticing it first, even though people tell me they don’t really see it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about this uneven spot at times when you're not looking in the mirror or seeing photos of yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. I mean, not constantly or anything, but even when I’m not looking at a mirror or a picture, I’ll catch myself thinking about that little uneven spot around my nose and cheek—especially if I’m at work under the bright lights. I start wondering if customers or my managers are noticing it first, even though people say they don’t really see it. I know it probably sounds kind of minor.\n\nDoctor: How often would you say you think about this uneven spot during a typical day?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know exactly. It’s not like every second, but probably a few times a day? Especially when I’m at work under the store lights, or if I know someone’s looking at me, I start thinking about that tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek. I kind of tell myself other people say they don’t see it, but it still feels like it’s what customers or managers notice first. Is that… is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Has thinking about this uneven spot around your nose and cheek ever interfered with your daily activities or relationships?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… not like it’s ruining everything, but at work it’s been kind of getting in the way. My manager has noticed I’m coming back from breaks late sometimes, and I guess I’ve been moving slower at the register because I get distracted thinking about it.\n\nI feel bad saying that because I don’t want it to sound like I’m not trying. I’m actually trying to get promoted, and I’m worried it’s making me look unreliable right when I need to seem dependable. Does that count as interfering?\n\nDoctor: Are you concerned about what other people think of you in general, or is it mostly about how you look?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mostly about how I look… like, not that I don’t care what people think at all, but the thing I came in for is I keep getting stuck on this really tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek. It’s worse in photos or under the store lights, and even when people tell me they don’t notice it, I still feel like it’s the first thing customers or managers see.\n\nI know that probably sounds kind of small, but… yeah, it’s mostly that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to stop thinking about the uneven spot around your nose and cheek, but find that you can't?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do try to stop sometimes. Like I’ll tell myself, “Okay, other people said they don’t even see it, just let it go,” but then if I’m under the store lights or I catch myself in a photo, I get stuck on it again.\n\nIt’s this tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek, and I know it probably sounds really small, but it starts feeling like it’s the first thing customers or managers notice. I don’t want to be dramatic about it, but… yeah, it’s hard to just not think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or upset when you think about this uneven spot around your nose and cheek?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound bigger than it is, but I do get upset about it. It’s mostly when I see it in photos or under the store lights, and then I start thinking everyone can see that uneven spot around my nose and cheek.\n\nEven when people say they don’t notice it, I still feel like it’s the first thing customers or managers are looking at. I know that probably sounds silly, but it does make me anxious sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to do something specific, like checking or touching, to make yourself feel better about the uneven spot around your nose and cheek?\n\nPatient: Um… yeah, I guess I do sometimes. It’s more like, on my breaks I’ll end up checking in the bathroom mirror until I feel like I can go back out.\n\nSometimes I fix my hair around that side too, and… I’ll compare myself to girls who come through my line, even though I know that’s probably not helpful. I don’t mean for it to take long, but sometimes I do take too long and I’m a little late getting back to the register.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your face in the mirror to stop yourself from thinking about the uneven spot?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, kind of. Sometimes I also compare myself to girls coming through my line, which I know probably isn’t great. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess it’s a little embarrassing, but I’ve been kind of stuck on this small uneven spot around my nose and cheek. It’s probably not that noticeable, and people tell me they don’t really see it, but in photos or under the lights at work, I feel like it’s the first thing customers or managers notice. I know that might sound silly, but it’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Is it specifically this uneven spot that bothers you, or do you feel self-conscious about your appearance in general?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mostly that specific spot, yeah… the little uneven area around my nose and cheek. I mean, I don’t think I’m, like, unhappy with everything about how I look or anything. It’s just in photos, or under the bright store lights, I get stuck on it and feel like customers or my managers are noticing it first, even though people tell me they don’t really see it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about this uneven spot at times when you're not looking in the mirror or seeing photos of yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. I mean, not constantly or anything, but even when I’m not looking at a mirror or a picture, I’ll catch myself thinking about that little uneven spot around my nose and cheek—especially if I’m at work under the bright lights. I start wondering if customers or my managers are noticing it first, even though people say they don’t really see it. I know it probably sounds kind of minor.\n\nDoctor: How often would you say you think about this uneven spot during a typical day?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know exactly. It’s not like every second, but probably a few times a day? Especially when I’m at work under the store lights, or if I know someone’s looking at me, I start thinking about that tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek. I kind of tell myself other people say they don’t see it, but it still feels like it’s what customers or managers notice first. Is that… is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Has thinking about this uneven spot around your nose and cheek ever interfered with your daily activities or relationships?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… not like it’s ruining everything, but at work it’s been kind of getting in the way. My manager has noticed I’m coming back from breaks late sometimes, and I guess I’ve been moving slower at the register because I get distracted thinking about it.\n\nI feel bad saying that because I don’t want it to sound like I’m not trying. I’m actually trying to get promoted, and I’m worried it’s making me look unreliable right when I need to seem dependable. Does that count as interfering?\n\nDoctor: Are you concerned about what other people think of you in general, or is it mostly about how you look?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mostly about how I look… like, not that I don’t care what people think at all, but the thing I came in for is I keep getting stuck on this really tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek. It’s worse in photos or under the store lights, and even when people tell me they don’t notice it, I still feel like it’s the first thing customers or managers see.\n\nI know that probably sounds kind of small, but… yeah, it’s mostly that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to stop thinking about the uneven spot around your nose and cheek, but find that you can't?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do try to stop sometimes. Like I’ll tell myself, “Okay, other people said they don’t even see it, just let it go,” but then if I’m under the store lights or I catch myself in a photo, I get stuck on it again.\n\nIt’s this tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek, and I know it probably sounds really small, but it starts feeling like it’s the first thing customers or managers notice. I don’t want to be dramatic about it, but… yeah, it’s hard to just not think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or upset when you think about this uneven spot around your nose and cheek?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound bigger than it is, but I do get upset about it. It’s mostly when I see it in photos or under the store lights, and then I start thinking everyone can see that uneven spot around my nose and cheek.\n\nEven when people say they don’t notice it, I still feel like it’s the first thing customers or managers are looking at. I know that probably sounds silly, but it does make me anxious sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to do something specific, like checking or touching, to make yourself feel better about the uneven spot around your nose and cheek?\n\nPatient: Um… yeah, I guess I do sometimes. It’s more like, on my breaks I’ll end up checking in the bathroom mirror until I feel like I can go back out.\n\nSometimes I fix my hair around that side too, and… I’ll compare myself to girls who come through my line, even though I know that’s probably not helpful. I don’t mean for it to take long, but sometimes I do take too long and I’m a little late getting back to the register.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your face in the mirror to stop yourself from thinking about the uneven spot?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, kind of. I don’t tell myself, like, “I have to do this,” but if I don’t check it, I keep thinking about it while I’m at the register.\n\nSo on my breaks I’ll go look in the bathroom mirror, fix my hair around that side a little, and then I feel like I can go back out. Kind of, yeah… I mean, I can avoid it sometimes if I really have to, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. But on my breaks, I usually end up going to the bathroom mirror to check my face and fix my hair around it.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess it’s a little embarrassing, but I’ve been kind of stuck on this small uneven spot around my nose and cheek. It’s probably not that noticeable, and people tell me they don’t really see it, but in photos or under the lights at work, I feel like it’s the first thing customers or managers notice. I know that might sound silly, but it’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Is it specifically this uneven spot that bothers you, or do you feel self-conscious about your appearance in general?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mostly that specific spot, yeah… the little uneven area around my nose and cheek. I mean, I don’t think I’m, like, unhappy with everything about how I look or anything. It’s just in photos, or under the bright store lights, I get stuck on it and feel like customers or my managers are noticing it first, even though people tell me they don’t really see it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about this uneven spot at times when you're not looking in the mirror or seeing photos of yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. I mean, not constantly or anything, but even when I’m not looking at a mirror or a picture, I’ll catch myself thinking about that little uneven spot around my nose and cheek—especially if I’m at work under the bright lights. I start wondering if customers or my managers are noticing it first, even though people say they don’t really see it. I know it probably sounds kind of minor.\n\nDoctor: How often would you say you think about this uneven spot during a typical day?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know exactly. It’s not like every second, but probably a few times a day? Especially when I’m at work under the store lights, or if I know someone’s looking at me, I start thinking about that tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek. I kind of tell myself other people say they don’t see it, but it still feels like it’s what customers or managers notice first. Is that… is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Has thinking about this uneven spot around your nose and cheek ever interfered with your daily activities or relationships?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… not like it’s ruining everything, but at work it’s been kind of getting in the way. My manager has noticed I’m coming back from breaks late sometimes, and I guess I’ve been moving slower at the register because I get distracted thinking about it.\n\nI feel bad saying that because I don’t want it to sound like I’m not trying. I’m actually trying to get promoted, and I’m worried it’s making me look unreliable right when I need to seem dependable. Does that count as interfering?\n\nDoctor: Are you concerned about what other people think of you in general, or is it mostly about how you look?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mostly about how I look… like, not that I don’t care what people think at all, but the thing I came in for is I keep getting stuck on this really tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek. It’s worse in photos or under the store lights, and even when people tell me they don’t notice it, I still feel like it’s the first thing customers or managers see.\n\nI know that probably sounds kind of small, but… yeah, it’s mostly that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to stop thinking about the uneven spot around your nose and cheek, but find that you can't?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do try to stop sometimes. Like I’ll tell myself, “Okay, other people said they don’t even see it, just let it go,” but then if I’m under the store lights or I catch myself in a photo, I get stuck on it again.\n\nIt’s this tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek, and I know it probably sounds really small, but it starts feeling like it’s the first thing customers or managers notice. I don’t want to be dramatic about it, but… yeah, it’s hard to just not think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or upset when you think about this uneven spot around your nose and cheek?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound bigger than it is, but I do get upset about it. It’s mostly when I see it in photos or under the store lights, and then I start thinking everyone can see that uneven spot around my nose and cheek.\n\nEven when people say they don’t notice it, I still feel like it’s the first thing customers or managers are looking at. I know that probably sounds silly, but it does make me anxious sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to do something specific, like checking or touching, to make yourself feel better about the uneven spot around your nose and cheek?\n\nPatient: Um… yeah, I guess I do sometimes. I don’t mean for it to take long, but sometimes I do take too long and I’m a little late getting back to the register.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your face in the mirror to stop yourself from thinking about the uneven spot?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, kind of. I don’t tell myself, like, “I have to do this,” but if I don’t check it, I keep thinking about it while I’m at the register.\n\nSo on my breaks I’ll go look in the bathroom mirror, fix my hair around that side a little, and then I feel like I can go back out. Yeah… I think I would have a hard time stopping, if I’m being honest. Like, I don’t want to sound dramatic, because it’s probably really small, but I get stuck on this uneven spot around my nose and cheek—especially in photos or under the store lights.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess it’s a little embarrassing, but I’ve been kind of stuck on this small uneven spot around my nose and cheek. It’s probably not that noticeable, and people tell me they don’t really see it, but in photos or under the lights at work, I feel like it’s the first thing customers or managers notice. I know that might sound silly, but it’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Is it specifically this uneven spot that bothers you, or do you feel self-conscious about your appearance in general?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mostly that specific spot, yeah… the little uneven area around my nose and cheek. I mean, I don’t think I’m, like, unhappy with everything about how I look or anything. It’s just in photos, or under the bright store lights, I get stuck on it and feel like customers or my managers are noticing it first, even though people tell me they don’t really see it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about this uneven spot at times when you're not looking in the mirror or seeing photos of yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. I mean, not constantly or anything, but even when I’m not looking at a mirror or a picture, I’ll catch myself thinking about that little uneven spot around my nose and cheek—especially if I’m at work under the bright lights. I start wondering if customers or my managers are noticing it first, even though people say they don’t really see it. I know it probably sounds kind of minor.\n\nDoctor: How often would you say you think about this uneven spot during a typical day?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know exactly. It’s not like every second, but probably a few times a day? Especially when I’m at work under the store lights, or if I know someone’s looking at me, I start thinking about that tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek. I kind of tell myself other people say they don’t see it, but it still feels like it’s what customers or managers notice first. Is that… is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Has thinking about this uneven spot around your nose and cheek ever interfered with your daily activities or relationships?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… not like it’s ruining everything, but at work it’s been kind of getting in the way. My manager has noticed I’m coming back from breaks late sometimes, and I guess I’ve been moving slower at the register because I get distracted thinking about it.\n\nI feel bad saying that because I don’t want it to sound like I’m not trying. I’m actually trying to get promoted, and I’m worried it’s making me look unreliable right when I need to seem dependable. Does that count as interfering?\n\nDoctor: Are you concerned about what other people think of you in general, or is it mostly about how you look?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mostly about how I look… like, not that I don’t care what people think at all, but the thing I came in for is I keep getting stuck on this really tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek. It’s worse in photos or under the store lights, and even when people tell me they don’t notice it, I still feel like it’s the first thing customers or managers see.\n\nI know that probably sounds kind of small, but… yeah, it’s mostly that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to stop thinking about the uneven spot around your nose and cheek, but find that you can't?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do try to stop sometimes. Like I’ll tell myself, “Okay, other people said they don’t even see it, just let it go,” but then if I’m under the store lights or I catch myself in a photo, I get stuck on it again.\n\nIt’s this tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek, and I know it probably sounds really small, but it starts feeling like it’s the first thing customers or managers notice. I don’t want to be dramatic about it, but… yeah, it’s hard to just not think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or upset when you think about this uneven spot around your nose and cheek?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound bigger than it is, but I do get upset about it. It’s mostly when I see it in photos or under the store lights, and then I start thinking everyone can see that uneven spot around my nose and cheek.\n\nEven when people say they don’t notice it, I still feel like it’s the first thing customers or managers are looking at. I know that probably sounds silly, but it does make me anxious sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to do something specific, like checking or touching, to make yourself feel better about the uneven spot around your nose and cheek?\n\nPatient: Um… yeah, I guess I do sometimes. Not like constantly, but on my breaks I’ll go into the bathroom and check my face in the mirror, kind of just to see if it looks as bad as I’m thinking.\n\nI’ll fix my hair around that side too, like maybe if it sits a certain way it won’t show as much. And… I don’t know if this sounds bad, but I catch myself comparing my face to girls who come through my line.\n\nSometimes I take longer than I mean to, and I’ve been a little late getting back to the register. I know that’s probably not great.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your face in the mirror a certain number of times to feel okay about the uneven spot?\n\nPatient: Not a certain number, no… I don’t think I’m counting it like that. It’s more like, on my breaks I’ll end up checking in the bathroom mirror until I feel like I can go back out.\n\nSometimes I fix my hair around that side too, and… I’ll compare myself to girls who come through my line, even though I know that’s probably not helpful. I don’t mean for it to take long, but sometimes I do take too long and I’m a little late getting back to the register.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your face in the mirror to stop yourself from thinking about the uneven spot?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, kind of. I don’t tell myself, like, “I have to do this,” but if I don’t check it, I keep thinking about it while I’m at the register.\n\nSo on my breaks I’ll go look in the bathroom mirror, fix my hair around that side a little, and then I feel like I can go back out. Sometimes I also compare myself to girls coming through my line, which I know probably isn’t great. I don’t mean for it to take that long, but a couple times I’ve been late getting back… which I feel bad about.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your face in the mirror is something you have to do, even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it kind of feels that way sometimes. I don’t really want to be doing it, especially at work, but on my breaks I end up going to the bathroom mirror and checking my face anyway.\n\nI’ll try to fix my hair around that side too, like maybe it’ll cover it better. And I know this probably isn’t the best thing, but I compare myself to girls who come through my line. Sometimes I take longer than I mean to and I’m late getting back to the register… which I feel bad about.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would get anxious or upset if you couldn't check your face in the mirror when you feel the need to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would get kind of anxious. Not like a huge meltdown or anything, but it would bother me, and I’d keep thinking about it.\n\nUsually on my breaks I just go check in the bathroom mirror and fix my hair around that side a little, and then I can go back out. If I couldn’t do that, I think I’d feel really distracted at the register. I know it sounds silly… I also catch myself comparing to girls who come through my line, and sometimes I take longer than I mean to and get back late.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control when you decide to check your face in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’d like to say I’m in control of it, because I do choose to go on my break and check. But honestly, once it’s in my head, it doesn’t feel totally like a normal choice.\n\nI’ll end up in the bathroom mirror looking at my face, fixing my hair around that spot, and then I feel a little better after. And… I guess I also compare myself to girls who come through my line. I know that probably sounds bad. Sometimes I take longer than I meant to and I’m late getting back to the register, which I’m not trying to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking your face in the mirror because you feel like you have to, or because you want to?\n\nPatient: I think… more because I feel like I have to, honestly. I don’t really want to spend my break doing that, but I end up going to the bathroom mirror to check my face and fix my hair around it.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to be difficult or anything… I just feel better if I check. And sometimes I guess I compare myself to girls who come through my line too. I don’t mean to take too long, but sometimes I do and I’m late getting back to the register.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your face in the mirror is something you cannot avoid doing?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yeah… I mean, I can avoid it sometimes if I really have to, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. But on my breaks, I usually end up going to the bathroom mirror to check my face and fix my hair around it.\n\nIt feels like once I start thinking about it, it’s hard to just let it go. And sometimes I’m in there longer than I meant to be, or I’m comparing myself to girls who come through my line… which I know probably isn’t great. Sometimes I end up late getting back to the register.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would be unable to stop thinking about the uneven spot if you couldn't check your face in the mirror?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would have a hard time stopping, if I’m being honest. Like, I don’t want to sound dramatic, because it’s probably really small, but I get stuck on this uneven spot around my nose and cheek—especially in photos or under the store lights.\n\nEven if someone says they don’t see it, I still feel like it’s the first thing customers or managers notice when they look at me. So if I couldn’t check, I think I’d just keep thinking about it and wondering how bad it looked.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Sometimes I take longer than I meant to and I’m late getting back to the register, which I’m not trying to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking your face in the mirror because you feel like you have to, or because you want to?\n\nPatient: I think… more because I feel like I have to, honestly. I don’t really want to spend my break doing that, but I end up going to the bathroom mirror to check my face and fix my hair around it.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to be difficult or anything… I just feel better if I check. And sometimes I guess I compare myself to girls who come through my line too. I don’t mean to take too long, but sometimes I do and I’m late getting back to the register.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your face in the mirror is something you cannot avoid doing?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yeah… I mean, I can avoid it sometimes if I really have to, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. But on my breaks, I usually end up going to the bathroom mirror to check my face and fix my hair around it.\n\nIt feels like once I start thinking about it, it’s hard to just let it go. And sometimes I’m in there longer than I meant to be, or I’m comparing myself to girls who come through my line… which I know probably isn’t great. Sometimes I end up late getting back to the register.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would be unable to stop thinking about the uneven spot if you couldn't check your face in the mirror?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would have a hard time stopping, if I’m being honest. Like, I don’t want to sound dramatic, because it’s probably really small, but I get stuck on this uneven spot around my nose and cheek—especially in photos or under the store lights.\n\nEven if someone says they don’t see it, I still feel like it’s the first thing customers or managers notice when they look at me. So if I couldn’t check, I think I’d just keep thinking about it and wondering how bad it looked.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess it’s a little embarrassing, but I’ve been kind of stuck on this small uneven spot around my nose and cheek. It’s probably not that noticeable, and people tell me they don’t really see it, but in photos or under the lights at work, I feel like it’s the first thing customers or managers notice. I know that might sound silly, but it’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Is it specifically this uneven spot that bothers you, or do you feel self-conscious about your appearance in general?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mostly that specific spot, yeah… the little uneven area around my nose and cheek. I mean, I don’t think I’m, like, unhappy with everything about how I look or anything. It’s just in photos, or under the bright store lights, I get stuck on it and feel like customers or my managers are noticing it first, even though people tell me they don’t really see it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about this uneven spot at times when you're not looking in the mirror or seeing photos of yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. I mean, not constantly or anything, but even when I’m not looking at a mirror or a picture, I’ll catch myself thinking about that little uneven spot around my nose and cheek—especially if I’m at work under the bright lights. I start wondering if customers or my managers are noticing it first, even though people say they don’t really see it. I know it probably sounds kind of minor.\n\nDoctor: How often would you say you think about this uneven spot during a typical day?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know exactly. It’s not like every second, but probably a few times a day? Especially when I’m at work under the store lights, or if I know someone’s looking at me, I start thinking about that tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek. I kind of tell myself other people say they don’t see it, but it still feels like it’s what customers or managers notice first. Is that… is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Has thinking about this uneven spot around your nose and cheek ever interfered with your daily activities or relationships?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… not like it’s ruining everything, but at work it’s been kind of getting in the way. My manager has noticed I’m coming back from breaks late sometimes, and I guess I’ve been moving slower at the register because I get distracted thinking about it.\n\nI feel bad saying that because I don’t want it to sound like I’m not trying. I’m actually trying to get promoted, and I’m worried it’s making me look unreliable right when I need to seem dependable. Does that count as interfering?\n\nDoctor: Are you concerned about what other people think of you in general, or is it mostly about how you look?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mostly about how I look… like, not that I don’t care what people think at all, but the thing I came in for is I keep getting stuck on this really tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek. It’s worse in photos or under the store lights, and even when people tell me they don’t notice it, I still feel like it’s the first thing customers or managers see.\n\nI know that probably sounds kind of small, but… yeah, it’s mostly that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to stop thinking about the uneven spot around your nose and cheek, but find that you can't?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do try to stop sometimes. Like I’ll tell myself, “Okay, other people said they don’t even see it, just let it go,” but then if I’m under the store lights or I catch myself in a photo, I get stuck on it again.\n\nIt’s this tiny uneven spot around my nose and cheek, and I know it probably sounds really small, but it starts feeling like it’s the first thing customers or managers notice. I don’t want to be dramatic about it, but… yeah, it’s hard to just not think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or upset when you think about this uneven spot around your nose and cheek?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound bigger than it is, but I do get upset about it. It’s mostly when I see it in photos or under the store lights, and then I start thinking everyone can see that uneven spot around my nose and cheek.\n\nEven when people say they don’t notice it, I still feel like it’s the first thing customers or managers are looking at. I know that probably sounds silly, but it does make me anxious sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to do something specific, like checking or touching, to make yourself feel better about the uneven spot around your nose and cheek?\n\nPatient: Um… yeah, I guess I do sometimes. Not like constantly, but on my breaks I’ll go into the bathroom and check my face in the mirror, kind of just to see if it looks as bad as I’m thinking.\n\nI’ll fix my hair around that side too, like maybe if it sits a certain way it won’t show as much. And… I don’t know if this sounds bad, but I catch myself comparing my face to girls who come through my line.\n\nSometimes I take longer than I mean to, and I’ve been a little late getting back to the register. I know that’s probably not great.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your face in the mirror a certain number of times to feel okay about the uneven spot?\n\nPatient: Not a certain number, no… I don’t think I’m counting it like that. It’s more like, on my breaks I’ll end up checking in the bathroom mirror until I feel like I can go back out.\n\nSometimes I fix my hair around that side too, and… I’ll compare myself to girls who come through my line, even though I know that’s probably not helpful. I don’t mean for it to take long, but sometimes I do take too long and I’m a little late getting back to the register.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your face in the mirror to stop yourself from thinking about the uneven spot?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, kind of. I don’t tell myself, like, “I have to do this,” but if I don’t check it, I keep thinking about it while I’m at the register.\n\nSo on my breaks I’ll go look in the bathroom mirror, fix my hair around that side a little, and then I feel like I can go back out. Sometimes I also compare myself to girls coming through my line, which I know probably isn’t great. I don’t mean for it to take that long, but a couple times I’ve been late getting back… which I feel bad about.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your face in the mirror is something you have to do, even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it kind of feels that way sometimes. I don’t really want to be doing it, especially at work, but on my breaks I end up going to the bathroom mirror and checking my face anyway.\n\nI’ll try to fix my hair around that side too, like maybe it’ll cover it better. And I know this probably isn’t the best thing, but I compare myself to girls who come through my line. Sometimes I take longer than I mean to and I’m late getting back to the register… which I feel bad about.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would get anxious or upset if you couldn't check your face in the mirror when you feel the need to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would get kind of anxious. Not like a huge meltdown or anything, but it would bother me, and I’d keep thinking about it.\n\nUsually on my breaks I just go check in the bathroom mirror and fix my hair around that side a little, and then I can go back out. If I couldn’t do that, I think I’d feel really distracted at the register. I know it sounds silly… I also catch myself comparing to girls who come through my line, and sometimes I take longer than I mean to and get back late.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control when you decide to check your face in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’d like to say I’m in control of it, because I do choose to go on my break and check. But honestly, once it’s in my head, it doesn’t feel totally like a normal choice.\n\nI’ll end up in the bathroom mirror looking at my face, fixing my hair around that spot, and then I feel a little better after. Sometimes I take longer than I meant to and I’m late getting back to the register, which I’m not trying to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking your face in the mirror because you feel like you have to, or because you want to?\n\nPatient: I think… more because I feel like I have to, honestly. And sometimes I guess I compare myself to girls who come through my line too. I don’t mean to take too long, but sometimes I do and I’m late getting back to the register.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your face in the mirror is something you cannot avoid doing?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yeah… I mean, I can avoid it sometimes if I really have to, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I know that probably sounds silly, but it does make me anxious sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to do something specific, like checking or touching, to make yourself feel better about the uneven spot around your nose and cheek?\n\nPatient: Um… yeah, I guess I do sometimes. Not like constantly, but on my breaks I’ll go into the bathroom and check my face in the mirror, kind of just to see if it looks as bad as I’m thinking.\n\nI’ll fix my hair around that side too, like maybe if it sits a certain way it won’t show as much. And… I don’t know if this sounds bad, but I catch myself comparing my face to girls who come through my line.\n\nSometimes I take longer than I mean to, and I’ve been a little late getting back to the register. I know that’s probably not great.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your face in the mirror a certain number of times to feel okay about the uneven spot?\n\nPatient: Not a certain number, no… I don’t think I’m counting it like that. It’s more like, on my breaks I’ll end up checking in the bathroom mirror until I feel like I can go back out.\n\nSometimes I fix my hair around that side too, and… I’ll compare myself to girls who come through my line, even though I know that’s probably not helpful. I don’t mean for it to take long, but sometimes I do take too long and I’m a little late getting back to the register.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your face in the mirror to stop yourself from thinking about the uneven spot?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, kind of. I don’t tell myself, like, “I have to do this,” but if I don’t check it, I keep thinking about it while I’m at the register.\n\nSo on my breaks I’ll go look in the bathroom mirror, fix my hair around that side a little, and then I feel like I can go back out. Sometimes I also compare myself to girls coming through my line, which I know probably isn’t great. I don’t mean for it to take that long, but a couple times I’ve been late getting back… which I feel bad about.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your face in the mirror is something you have to do, even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it kind of feels that way sometimes. I don’t really want to be doing it, especially at work, but on my breaks I end up going to the bathroom mirror and checking my face anyway.\n\nI’ll try to fix my hair around that side too, like maybe it’ll cover it better. And I know this probably isn’t the best thing, but I compare myself to girls who come through my line. Sometimes I take longer than I mean to and I’m late getting back to the register… which I feel bad about.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would get anxious or upset if you couldn't check your face in the mirror when you feel the need to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would get kind of anxious. Not like a huge meltdown or anything, but it would bother me, and I’d keep thinking about it.\n\nUsually on my breaks I just go check in the bathroom mirror and fix my hair around that side a little, and then I can go back out. If I couldn’t do that, I think I’d feel really distracted at the register. I know it sounds silly… I also catch myself comparing to girls who come through my line, and sometimes I take longer than I mean to and get back late.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control when you decide to check your face in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’d like to say I’m in control of it, because I do choose to go on my break and check. But honestly, once it’s in my head, it doesn’t feel totally like a normal choice.\n\nI’ll end up in the bathroom mirror looking at my face, fixing my hair around that spot, and then I feel a little better after. And… I guess I also compare myself to girls who come through my line. I know that probably sounds bad. Sometimes I take longer than I meant to and I’m late getting back to the register, which I’m not trying to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking your face in the mirror because you feel like you have to, or because you want to?\n\nPatient: I think… more because I feel like I have to, honestly. I don’t really want to spend my break doing that, but I end up going to the bathroom mirror to check my face and fix my hair around it.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to be difficult or anything… I just feel better if I check. And sometimes I guess I compare myself to girls who come through my line too. I don’t mean to take too long, but sometimes I do and I’m late getting back to the register.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your face in the mirror is something you cannot avoid doing?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yeah… I mean, I can avoid it sometimes if I really have to, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. But on my breaks, I usually end up going to the bathroom mirror to check my face and fix my hair around it.\n\nIt feels like once I start thinking about it, it’s hard to just let it go. And sometimes I’m in there longer than I meant to be, or I’m comparing myself to girls who come through my line… which I know probably isn’t great. Sometimes I end up late getting back to the register.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would be unable to stop thinking about the uneven spot if you couldn't check your face in the mirror?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would have a hard time stopping, if I’m being honest. Like, I don’t want to sound dramatic, because it’s probably really small, but I get stuck on this uneven spot around my nose and cheek—especially in photos or under the store lights.\n\nEven if someone says they don’t see it, I still feel like it’s the first thing customers or managers notice when they look at me. So if I couldn’t check, I think I’d just keep thinking about it and wondering how bad it looked.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your face in the mirror is something that you feel forced to do, even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think “forced” is maybe a strong word, but it does feel that way sometimes. Like on my breaks, I’ll tell myself I’m just going to use the bathroom, and then I end up checking my face in the mirror and fixing my hair around it.\n\nI don’t really want to take forever or anything, but I kind of feel like I have to make sure it looks okay. And sometimes I compare myself to girls who come through my line, which is embarrassing to say. I’ve been late getting back to the register because I stayed in there too long… not all the time, but it has happened.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I don’t mean for it to take long, but sometimes I do take too long and I’m a little late getting back to the register.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your face in the mirror to stop yourself from thinking about the uneven spot?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, kind of. It’s more like, on my breaks I’ll end up checking in the bathroom mirror until I feel like I can go back out.\n\nSometimes I fix my hair around that side too, and… I’ll compare myself to girls who come through my line, even though I know that’s probably not helpful. I don’t mean for it to take long, but sometimes I do take too long and I’m a little late getting back to the register.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your face in the mirror to stop yourself from thinking about the uneven spot?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, kind of. I don’t tell myself, like, “I have to do this,” but if I don’t check it, I keep thinking about it while I’m at the register.\n\nSo on my breaks I’ll go look in the bathroom mirror, fix my hair around that side a little, and then I feel like I can go back out. Sometimes I also compare myself to girls coming through my line, which I know probably isn’t great. I don’t mean for it to take that long, but a couple times I’ve been late getting back… which I feel bad about.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your face in the mirror is something you have to do, even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it kind of feels that way sometimes. I don’t really want to be doing it, especially at work, but on my breaks I end up going to the bathroom mirror and checking my face anyway.\n\nI’ll try to fix my hair around that side too, like maybe it’ll cover it better. And I know this probably isn’t the best thing, but I compare myself to girls who come through my line. Sometimes I take longer than I mean to and I’m late getting back to the register… which I feel bad about.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would get anxious or upset if you couldn't check your face in the mirror when you feel the need to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would get kind of anxious. Not like a huge meltdown or anything, but it would bother me, and I’d keep thinking about it.\n\nUsually on my breaks I just go check in the bathroom mirror and fix my hair around that side a little, and then I can go back out. If I couldn’t do that, I think I’d feel really distracted at the register. I know it sounds silly… I also catch myself comparing to girls who come through my line, and sometimes I take longer than I mean to and get back late.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control when you decide to check your face in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’d like to say I’m in control of it, because I do choose to go on my break and check. But honestly, once it’s in my head, it doesn’t feel totally like a normal choice.\n\nI’ll end up in the bathroom mirror looking at my face, fixing my hair around that spot, and then I feel a little better after. And… I guess I also compare myself to girls who come through my line. I know that probably sounds bad. Sometimes I take longer than I meant to and I’m late getting back to the register, which I’m not trying to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking your face in the mirror because you feel like you have to, or because you want to?\n\nPatient: I think… more because I feel like I have to, honestly. I don’t really want to spend my break doing that, but I end up going to the bathroom mirror to check my face and fix my hair around it.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to be difficult or anything… I just feel better if I check. And sometimes I guess I compare myself to girls who come through my line too. I don’t mean to take too long, but sometimes I do and I’m late getting back to the register.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your face in the mirror is something you cannot avoid doing?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yeah… I mean, I can avoid it sometimes if I really have to, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. But on my breaks, I usually end up going to the bathroom mirror to check my face and fix my hair around it.\n\nIt feels like once I start thinking about it, it’s hard to just let it go. And sometimes I’m in there longer than I meant to be, or I’m comparing myself to girls who come through my line… which I know probably isn’t great. Sometimes I end up late getting back to the register.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would be unable to stop thinking about the uneven spot if you couldn't check your face in the mirror?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would have a hard time stopping, if I’m being honest. Like, I don’t want to sound dramatic, because it’s probably really small, but I get stuck on this uneven spot around my nose and cheek—especially in photos or under the store lights.\n\nEven if someone says they don’t see it, I still feel like it’s the first thing customers or managers notice when they look at me. So if I couldn’t check, I think I’d just keep thinking about it and wondering how bad it looked.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your face in the mirror is something that you feel forced to do, even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think “forced” is maybe a strong word, but it does feel that way sometimes. Like on my breaks, I’ll tell myself I’m just going to use the bathroom, and then I end up checking my face in the mirror and fixing my hair around it.\n\nI don’t really want to take forever or anything, but I kind of feel like I have to make sure it looks okay. And sometimes I compare myself to girls who come through my line, which is embarrassing to say. I’ve been late getting back to the register because I stayed in there too long… not all the time, but it has happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your face in the mirror is something that you cannot stop doing, even if you want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I can stop if I really have to, like if someone’s waiting on me or I’m already late. But it doesn’t always feel like I’m choosing it, if that makes sense.\n\nOn my breaks I’ll end up in the bathroom mirror checking my face, and then fixing my hair around it so it looks less noticeable. And sometimes I start comparing myself to girls who came through my line, which I know probably isn’t helpful. I don’t mean to take that long, but a few times I’ve stayed in there enough that I’m late getting back to the register.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would experience significant distress if you were prevented from checking your face in the mirror?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would get pretty upset, honestly. I’d try not to make it a big deal, because I know it probably sounds kind of silly, but if I couldn’t check it, I’d be thinking about it the whole time.\n\nLike on breaks, I usually end up in the bathroom mirror checking that spot and fixing my hair around it so it doesn’t stand out as much. And if I couldn’t do that, I’d feel really uncomfortable going back to the register. Sometimes I already take too long and get back late, which I know isn’t good. I just feel like I need to make sure it looks okay first.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
